=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer side, representing the edge length of a square with corners at (0, 0), (0, side), (side, 0), and (side, side) on a Cartesian plane.
You are also given a positive integer k and a 2D integer array points, where points[i] = [x_i, y_i] represents the coordinate of a point lying on the boundary of the square.
You need to select k elements among points such that the minimum Manhattan distance between any two points is maximized.
Return the maximum possible minimum Manhattan distance between the selected k points.
The Manhattan Distance between two cells (x_i, y_i) and (x_j, y_j) is |x_i - x_j| + |y_i - y_j|.
 
Example 1:

Input: side = 2, points = [[0,2],[2,0],[2,2],[0,0]], k = 4
Output: 2
Explanation:

Select all four points.

Example 2:

Input: side = 2, points = [[0,0],[1,2],[2,0],[2,2],[2,1]], k = 4
Output: 1
Explanation:

Select the points (0, 0), (2, 0), (2, 2), and (2, 1).

Example 3:

Input: side = 2, points = [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2],[1,2],[2,0],[2,2],[2,1]], k = 5
Output: 1
Explanation:

Select the points (0, 0), (0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2), and (2, 2).

 
Constraints:

1 <= side <= 10^9
4 <= points.length <= min(4 * side, 15 * 10^3)
points[i] == [xi, yi]
The input is generated such that:
	
points[i] lies on the boundary of the square.
All points[i] are unique.


4 <= k <= min(25, points.length)

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Option 1: For each check(d), build arr = t + [v+L for v in t] length 2n. Compute nxt array length 2n using two pointers.

Pseudo:

def can(d):
    if d == 0: return True
    arr = t + [x + L for x in t]
    m = 2*n
    nxt = [m] * m
    j = 0
    for i in range(m):
        if j < i+1:
            j = i+1
        while j < m and arr[j] - arr[i] < d:
            j += 1
        nxt[i] = j
    # For each start original index 0..n-1
    for s in range(n):
        cur = s
        ok = True
        # We need select k points: start plus k-1 jumps
        for _ in range(k-1):
            cur = nxt[cur]
            if cur >= m:
                ok = False
                break
        if ok:
            if arr[s] + L - arr[cur] >= d:
                return True
    return False

Now, reduction D<d iff C<d for d<=side. Let's prove:
Let C be circular perimeter distance. If C < d <= s, then the shorter perimeter arc has length <s. Does that imply points are on same or adjacent sides? The shorter arc length <s means it cannot contain two corners? It could contain one corner at most; so points lie on same side or adjacent sides. Along that arc, Manhattan distance equals arc length (because moving along boundary in one axis direction? Actually if same side, yes; if adjacent sides, path consists of two perpendicular segments, length = |dx|+|dy| = D). Thus D=C<d.
Conversely, if D<d<=s, then D<s. Need show C=D. If points are on opposite sides, D >= s? Let's verify for any opposite sides (left/right or top/bottom), Manhattan distance is at least s. Yes, because x difference =s for left/right, or y difference=s for top/bottom. Thus D<s implies not opposite sides, so same/adjacent, and D=C. Therefore D<d iff C<d. Good.

Thus for d<=side, pairwise Manhattan >=d iff circular perimeter distance >=d.

Let's think about edge cases:
- Points may include corners; perimeter coordinate assignment must be consistent and sorted. For (0,0) t=0; for (0, side) top-left t=3s; for (side,0) t=s; for (side,side) t=2s. Good.

- can(d) uses t list sorted. We can precompute t once in maxDistance.

def can(d):
    if d == 0: return True
    arr = t + [x + L for x in t]
    m = 2*n
    nxt = [m] * m
    j = 0
    for i in range(m):
        if j < i + 1:
            j = i + 1
        while j < m and arr[j] - arr[i] < d:
            j += 1
        nxt[i] = j
    # optional: if nxt[s] >= s+n for all? no
    for s in range(n):
        cur = s
        limit = s + n
        ok = True
        for _ in range(k - 1):
            cur = nxt[cur]
            if cur >= limit:
                ok = False
                break
        if ok and arr[s] + L - arr[cur] >= d:
            return True
    return False

Now, let's revisit the reduction D<d iff C<d with d<=side. We should ensure C is circular perimeter distance, not "Manhattan distance along perimeter" for adjacent sides. If C<d<=s, then path through common corner <d<=s, so C=path. If path through common corner >2s? impossible. If points are on adjacent sides but the shorter perimeter arc might be the other path through the other three corners, not the common corner? For adjacent sides, the two perimeter arcs: one through the common corner length = a+b (where a,b distances to common corner), the other through the other three corners length = 4s - (a+b). Since a+b <=2s, the other >=2s. Thus the shorter arc is always the common corner path (unless a+b=2s, both 2s). So C = a+b = D. Good.

For same side, C = distance along side = D. For opposite sides, D may be less than C but D>=s. Since d<=s, D<d impossible. If D>=d, C may be >=? Could C<d while D>=d? If C<d<=s, then points are same/adjacent and D=C<d, contradiction. So C>=d. Thus equivalence.

Let's write code:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        s = side
        L = 4 * s
        t = []
        for x, y in points:
            if y == 0:
                t.append(x)
            elif x == s:
                t.append(s + y)
            elif y == s:
                t.append(2*s + (s - x))
            else:  # x == 0
                t.append(3*s + (s - y))  # or 4*s - y
        t.sort()
        n = len(t)

        def can(d: int) -> bool:
            if d == 0:
                return True
            arr = t + [v + L for v in t]
            m = 2 * n
            nxt = [m] * m
            j = 0
            # compute next index with arr[next] - arr[i] >= d
            for i in range(m):
                if j < i + 1:
                    j = i + 1
                while j < m and arr[j] - arr[i] < d:
                    j += 1
                nxt[i] = j

            # try each starting point
            for start in range(n):
                cur = start
                limit = start + n
                ok = True
                # need k points: start + (k-1) jumps
                for _ in range(k - 1):
                    cur = nxt[cur]
                    if cur >= limit:
                        ok = False
                        break
                if ok and arr[start] + L - arr[cur] >= d:
                    return True
            return False

        lo, hi = 1, s
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi + 1) // 2
            if can(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid - 1
        return lo

But our greedy ensures adjacent gaps >=d. Is it possible that two non-adjacent points have circular distance <d even though adjacent gaps >=d if one arc contains one gap and the other contains many? Let's verify for k=4, pair p1,p3: arc p1->p3 contains p2, gaps g1,g2 (two). other p3->p1 contains p4, gaps g3,g4 (two). For pair p1,p3 in k=5: arc p1->p3 contains p2, gaps g1,g2; other p3->p1 contains p4,p5, gaps g3,g4,g5. Both at least two. In general, if two points are not adjacent, each of the two arcs between them contains at least one selected point (otherwise they would be adjacent). An arc with at least one selected point consists of at least two gaps (from one endpoint to that point and from that point to the other endpoint). Thus each arc length >=2d. Therefore circular distance >=2d >=d. Good. So adjacent gaps suffice.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        s = side
        L = 4 * s

        # Perimeter coordinate, clockwise from (0,0):
        # bottom -> right -> top -> left
        t = []
        for x, y in points:
            if y == 0:          # bottom
                t.append(x)
            elif x == s:        # right
                t.append(s + y)
            elif y == s:        # top
                t.append(2 * s + (s - x))
            else:               # left
                t.append(3 * s + (s - y))

        t.sort()
        n = len(t)
        arr = t + [v + L for v in t]
        m = 2 * n

        def can(d: int) -> bool:
            if d == 0:
                return True

            # nxt[i] = first index j > i with arr[j] - arr[i] >= d
            nxt = [m] * m
            j = 0
            for i in range(m):
                if j < i + 1:
                    j = i + 1
                while j < m and arr[j] - arr[i] < d:
                    j += 1
                nxt[i] = j

            # Try every point as the first selected point.
            for start in range(n):
                cur = start
                limit = start + n  # do not select the starting point again
                ok = True

                for _ in range(k - 1):
                    cur = nxt[cur]
                    if cur >= limit:
                        ok = False
                        break

                if ok and arr[start] + L - arr[cur] >= d:
                    return True

            return False

        # For k >= 4, the answer is always at most side.
        lo, hi = 1, side
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi + 1) // 2
            if can(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid - 1

        return lo
```
